## Document 006: 10月金融数据点评：如何看社融增速回升？

Source PDF: all report pdf/10月金融数据点评：如何看社融增速回升？.pdf
Report source: 联储证券
Date: 2026-11-16
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

证券研究报告 
宏观经济点评 2026 年 11 月 16 日

请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明

10 月金融数据点评：如何看社融增速回升？

[Table_Author] 
 沈夏宜 分析师  魏争 分析师

Email:shenxiayi@lczq.com Email:weizheng@lczq.com  
证书:S1320523020004 证书:S1320524100001

### Page 2

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 2 
增 6983 亿元，是当月社融的主要支撑。一是去年同期到期规模较高，形
成较低基数。今年 3 月以来，月均国债发行稳定在 1.3 万亿左右，国债净
融资规模与当月到期规模关系较大；二是专项债发行仍保持一定强度，当
月新增专项债 3280 亿元，同比多增 406 亿元。 
企业债券、委托贷款、外币贷款均同比多增。当月新增企业债券 3446 亿
元，同比多增 946 亿元。当月债券利率下行，推动更多企业“以债替贷”
的融资转变；当月新增委托贷款 1862 亿元，同比多增 208 亿元。新型政
策性金融工具在 10 月全部投放完毕，集中投放对委托贷款形成支撑；当
月新增外币贷款 95 亿，同比多增 295 亿。10 月国内进口同比增长 
41.6%，进口付汇需求较强。此外，人民币汇率偏强亦对外币贷款需求形
成一定支撑。 
M1 增速回升。当月 M1 同比增长 4.4%，较上月回升 1.2 个百分点。一是
去年同期基数偏低；二是新型政策性金融工具集中投放，以及 PSL 利率
下调、支持扩围，对企业活期资金形成一定支撑；三是当月人民币延续升
值趋势，持续推动企业结汇意愿。 
M2 增速小幅回落。10 月 M2 同比增长 7.1%，较上月回落 0.1 个百分
点。一是当月社融主要增量来自于政府部门，但当月财政存款同比多增
6800 亿，向企业和居民部门释放偏慢；二是信贷增速续降，存款派生效
应偏弱。 
社融增速有望保持平稳。往前看，一是货币端，10 月末央行优化公开市
场操作机制，将隔夜逆回购转为日常操作，强化短端利率调控，有助于稳
定银行体系流动性；二是需求端，10 月 19 日，发改委等部门出台消费基
础设施建设指导意见，支持商业、文旅、交通、养老等领域设施建设，有
望拓宽相关领域融资需求；三是财政端，地方政府债务结存限额发行使用
进入年末收官阶段，叠加前期政策资金逐步形成实物工作量，项目配套融
资有望跟进，带动信贷融资边际修复。

风险提示：宏观经济不及预期，地产销售不及预期，美国货币政策超预
期，地缘政治风险超预期。

## Document 008: 10月金融数据点评：高增的政府债融资与新高的财政存款

Source PDF: all report pdf/10月金融数据点评：高增的政府债融资与新高的财政存款.pdf
Report source: 国信证券
Date: 2026-11-15
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

事项：
10 月份，新增社会融资规模 1.15万亿元，同比多增 3333亿元；人民币贷款净减少 1000亿元，同比少增
3200亿元。社融存量同比增长 7.2%，M2同比增长 7.1%。
评论：
图1：金融数据分项一览
资料来源：Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理
结论：总体来看，10 月社融仍然靠政府债支撑，实体融资较弱。社融存量增速小幅回升，但新增信贷转
负，居民和企业贷款同步净减少。M1 回升、M2 小幅回落，两者剪刀差收窄；政府债融资放量与财政存款
上升并存，财政存款再创新高。
展望后续，年末信贷仍面临实体融资需求不足与政策托底之间的拉锯。新型政策性金融工具项目拉动配套
贷款投放，叠加政府债券后续仍有较高发行预期，社融存量增速短期或趋稳，预计全年增速在 7.1%左右。
预计信贷持续偏弱，后续关注财政资金投放和非银贷款高增的持续性。

### Page 2

具体看，10 月数据：
社融同比多增，政府债是主要支撑。10月社融同比多增 3333亿元，其中政府债同比多增 7017亿元。扣除
政府债券后的非政府融资净减少 358亿元，同比少增 3684 亿元。
信贷净减少，居民和企业端同步走弱。10月人民币贷款同比少增 3200亿元，贷款余额增速降至 4.7%；居
民和企业贷款分别净减少 4300 亿元和 1600亿元，非银贷款多增有一定对冲，企业端主要是票据拖累。
总体来看，实体信用需求较弱，关注财政资金投放。M1 回升带动剪刀差收窄，但部分受低基数影响。财政
存款创新高的同时，非政府债的社融增速续创新低，后续观察财政资金投放和政策性工具能否带动非政府
融资修复。
解读：
 社融：政府债超过总量，非政府债净融资转负
社融主要靠政府债拉动。10 月新增社融 1.15 万亿元，低于市场预期（1.33 万亿元），较 9 月的 3.55 万
亿元季节性回落，同比多增 3333亿元，但低于过去五年同期 1.32万亿元的均值；社融存量同比增长 7.2%，
较上月回升 0.1个百分点。结构上，政府债券净融资 1.19万亿元，同比多增 7017亿元，超过当月社融总

量，是同比多增的主要来源。各主要科目同比增量排序为：政府债券（+7017 亿元）、非标（+844亿元）、
直接融资（+619亿元）、外币贷款（+296亿元）、人民币贷款（-5741 亿元）。
图2：社融的季节性 图3：新增社融及结构
资料来源:Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源:Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理
非政府融资转为净减少。扣除政府债后，10月非政府融资为-358 亿元，同比少增 3684亿元：贷款减少 5895
亿元，是主要拖累；非标融资减少 242 亿元，其中委托贷款增加 1860 亿元、同比多增 206 亿元，主要是
政策性金融工具集中落地拉动，信托贷款减少 415亿元、同比多减 570 亿元，未贴现银行承兑汇票减少 1687
亿元、同比少减 1208 亿元；直接融资增加 3814 亿元，其中企业债券融资增加 3468 亿元、同比多增 968
亿元，境内股票融资 346 亿元、同比少增 349 亿元。政府债券净融资 1.19万亿元，继续承担信用托底作
用。
我们测算，10 月非政府债融资存量同比增速录得 5.2%，较前值（5.3%）延续下行，再度刷新低点。剔除

政府债支撑后，实体经济的内生融资需求仍在持续走弱，意味着利率层面尚缺乏反弹动能。

### Page 3

图4：非政府融资增速仍处低位 图5：政策性金融工具带动委托贷款高增
资料来源:Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源:Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理
 信贷：实体部门净缩表
信贷余额增速继续下探。10月人民币贷款净减少 1000亿元，低于市场普遍预期的约 600亿元，同比少增
3200亿元；贷款余额同比增长 4.7%，较上月回落 0.1 个百分点，继续创有数据以来新低。
居民部门缩表继续。10月居民贷款净减少 4300亿元，同比多减 696亿元；短期贷款减少 2700亿元，同比
少减 166 亿元，中长期贷款减少 1563 亿元，同比多减 863 亿元。9 月暂停一个月后，10 月居民部门开始
继续缩表，购房贷款贴息政策尚未扭转居民中长贷的净偿还状态，政策效果仍待释放。
企业贷款由增转减。10 月企业贷款净减少 1600亿元，同比多减 5100 亿元；票据融资减少 3117亿元，而
上年同期增加 5006亿元，同比少增 8123亿元，季初月冲量下降，是企业贷款走弱的主要原因。企业短贷

和中长贷分别增加 100 亿元和 1600 亿元，同比分别多增 2000 亿元和 1300 亿元，显示企业经营性融资仍
有一定韧性。非银金融机构贷款增加 3470亿元，同比多增 2534亿元。
图6：信贷的季节性 图7：新增人民币贷款及结构
资料来源:WIND，国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源:WIND，国信证券经济研究所整理

### Page 4

图8：企业中长贷的季节性 图9：新增人民币贷款分项结构对比
资料来源:WIND，国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源:WIND，国信证券经济研究所整理
 货币与存款：财政存款再创新高
M1回升，剪刀差收窄。10 月 M2 同比增长 7.1%，较上月回落 0.1个百分点，低于市场预期的 7.2%；M1同
比增长 4.4%，回升 1.2个百分点，与 M2的剪刀差收窄至 2.7个百分点。M1 回升部分受去年同期基数回落
支撑，资金活化是否持续改善仍需观察。
存款端，居民和企业存款季节性流出，财政存款明显上升。10月新增人民币存款 1.21万亿元，同比多增
6000亿元：居民存款减少 9200亿元、同比少减 4200亿元；企业存款减少 1.25万亿元、同比多减 1647 亿
元；非银存款增加 1.58万亿元、同比少增 2700亿元；财政存款增加 1.40万亿元、同比多增 6800亿元。
目前财政存款已高达 9.4万亿，超过去年同期（7.7万亿）约 1.7万亿，在加强缴税与政府债放量背景下，
资金落地偏慢。按“政府债净融资-财政存款增加”估算，10 月广义财政缺口约-2100 亿元，可能预示支

出端尚未同步放量。
图10：广义货币增速 图11：社融与 M2剪刀差
资料来源:Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源:Wind，国信证券经济研究所整理

 风险提示
海外流动性收紧超预期，增量政策效果不及预期。

 投資評價與建議 
目標價調升至 1,000 元：(1) 3Q26 ABU 獲利改善優於預期，帶動公司平均毛利率
升至 42.2%(+2.2pts QoQ)且具延續力，(2) 產能建置加速，推動 2027 年獲利提
升，(3) 購置三峽廠，打開 DBU 擴產瓶頸。目標價隨 2028 年 EPS 調整，由 860
元(33x 2028 EPSF)上修至 1,000 元(32x 2028 EPSF)。

ESG 評析 
中砂企業永續評鑑整體為 A 等，於 SASB 產業永續議題與跨產業五大永續面向平
均得分分別為 A 等與 A 等。 
SinoPac+ ESG 評鑑系統評等

資料來源：SinoPac+ ESG 評等 (依循 2025 SinoPac+企業永續評鑑方法學) 
註 1：資誠永續發展服務股份有限公司僅於ＥＳＧ評鑑系統方法學建置過程中，就評分指標提供
專業意見，對於評分結果及評估報告內容之完整性及真實性，不負擔保責任，亦不對閱讀或使用
本評估報告之第三方負任何責任。 
註 2：永豐投顧已獲 SASB 授權，於研究中使用 SASB 重大性地圖之一般議題分類及揭露主題。
SinoPac Securities Investment Service licenses and applies the SASB Materiality Map® 
General Issue Categories and Disclosure Topics in our work.

### Page 2

2

中砂為先進製程鑽石碟第一把交椅：中砂成立於 1953 年，2005 年上市，目前資
本額約 14.6 億元，早期業務專注於傳統砂輪，後取得鑽石碟技術專利授權，並因
應晶圓代工廠客戶在地化策略，切入半導體領域。中砂主要有三大事業部，砂輪事
業部、鑽石事業部、晶圓事業部。3Q26 營收組成為 ABU(含砂輪子公司如鴻記、
KTC、MKS、MGT) 23%、DBU 32%、SBU 45%。中砂總部位於新北市鶯歌區，
砂輪、鑽石相關產品主要在鶯歌、樹林、湖口廠生產，而再生/測試晶圓主要在新
竹竹北、竹南廠生產，另在日本有 1 座廠房、泰國有 2 座廠房，另已在三峽新採
購 1 座廠房。客戶涵蓋半導體/記憶體製造商如台積電、美光、聯電、意法半導體、
英特爾，以及載板廠欣興等。 
3Q26 毛利率優於預期：中砂第三季稅後 EPS 3.91 元(+12.8%QoQ，+79.8%YoY)，
優於我們預期的 3.78 元，主因毛利率 42.2%季增約 2.2 個百分點高於預期，係來
自(1) 鑽石事業部營收占比及自身毛利率改善，係受到客戶惠先進製程出貨比重增
加，帶動高接產品放大，(2) ABU 產品組合改善，來自鑽石砂輪營收占比由 40%提
升至約 50%。 
加速擴產並改善獲利：我們認為 4Q26 的毛利率仍有提升空間，係考量(1) 先進製
程及 HBM 對鑽石碟產品用量增加，且公司產能由 3Q26 6.5 萬顆/月提升至 4Q26 
8 萬顆/月(原規劃 7 萬顆/月)，2nm/A14 用鑽石碟出貨量的增加將帶動 DBU 佔比
提升及獲利改善，(2) 部分測試晶圓及再生晶圓產品自 4Q26 起對所有客戶漲價約
10%，加上測試晶圓比重回落，可改善獲利率，(3) ABU 7 月漲價的效果可在 4Q26
顯現，加上鑽石砂輪、晶圓倒角砂輪、鑽石鍍膜等高階工具比重提升。另外，研究
部認為 2027 年中砂獲利成長動能仍將來自 DBU 的成長，目前公司規劃將鑽石碟
產能提升到明年底的 11 萬顆/月(+37.5%YoY)，以因應記憶體和美系 IDM 廠的擴
大採用、成熟製程客戶的產能擴增、以及晶圓代工廠先進製程積極擴產，同時預期
公司將適時與客戶們共同分擔生產成本的增加；SBU 則透過改造部分產線，並增
加表面缺陷檢測設備，來滿足客戶 HBM 對於特殊晶圓的需求。 
下一站，半導體用砂輪：由於既有樹林廠難以滿足客戶對鑽石碟中期需求，中砂於
今日董事會通過投入不超過 5.4 億新台幣於三峽購置廠房，用於擴增鑽石碟及半導
體廠用的鑽石砂輪產能，因應 CoWoS/SoIC/HBM 等先進封裝和晶背供電應用，
我們認為有望於 2028 年起貢獻營收並改善 DBU 產品組合，並隨 HPC 產品(及搭
配的記憶體)封裝規格提升和製程轉進而成長。 
再次上修預估：研究部預估中砂 2026 年全年營收 103.2 億(+26.6%YoY)，毛利率
40.4%，稅後淨利 21.9 億(+61.0%YoY)，EPS 14.60 元(原估 14.47 元)，獲利上調
主因 ABU 獲利率改善速度優於預期。2027 年營收 133.6 億(+29.5%YoY)，毛利
率 45.4%，稅後淨利 33.2 億(+51.5%YoY)，EPS 22.08 元(原估 20.26 元)。2028
年營收 174.2 億(+30.4%YoY)，毛利率 47.8%，稅後淨利 47.6 億(+43.3%YoY)，
EPS 31.65 元(原估 26.05 元)。

市場焦點
美國
• 10月S&P Global綜合PMI初值由58.4降至57.6，雖較前月回落，但仍處高檔區間；服務業動
能略為放緩，製造業PMI則升至56.0，創近4年半新高。新訂單增速加快，顯示企業需求、投
資及庫存回補動能仍具韌性，然而企業售價與投入成本同步上升，反映需求改善、供應鏈延
遲及成本轉嫁效應持續存在，通膨降溫進程仍具不確定性。整體而言，市場關注焦點轉向通
膨與利率走勢，高利率環境恐維持較長時間，進而推高股債資產評價波動。
中國
• 中國國家統計局公布第三季GDP年增率4.5%，優於市場預期，在出口與科技製造業帶動下，
短期成長可望維持穩定，但若內需未能有效改善，經濟成長動能恐難全面擴散，建議聚焦在
半導體、AI硬體、伺服器供應鏈，及高端製造相關產業，至於消費、房地產及傳統內需類股，

仍需觀察後續刺激政策與消費信心回升情況。
法國
政局動盪
• 法國金融市場因各地抗議活動持續擴大，面臨股債雙跌的局面。市場憂心法國政府可能擴大支
出以平息抗議，加劇財政可持續性疑慮。法國10年期公債殖利率一度突破5%，法德利差亦擴
大至歐債危機以來高點。不過，歐洲央行系統性壓力綜合指標仍處於歷史相對低檔，遠低於歐
元區主權債務危機期間，短期內演變為新一輪主權債務危機的風險仍相對有限。

顧
美國PMI維持強勁擴張，經濟成長續航但通膨壓力再升溫
• 企業需求強勁支撐美國景氣維持高檔擴張：美國10月綜合PMI初值由58.4降至57.6，雖略低於前月，但仍處於近五年來
的高位區間，顯示企業活動持續高速擴張；其中服務業PMI為57.9、製造業PMI升至56.0並創53個月新高，新訂單增速
更創逾4年半最佳表現，反映內需穩健、出口需求改善與企業補庫存效應同步發酵，美國第四季經濟動能仍具韌性。
• 成本上升與供應瓶頸推升通膨風險：企業售價漲幅創2022年8月以來最高，能源、運輸、金屬原料及薪資成本同步上揚，
加上供應鏈交期延長與訂單積壓擴大，使企業得以持續轉嫁成本，暗示未來數月消費者通膨可能再度偏離聯準會目標區
間，市場對利率維持較高水準的預期恐因此升溫。
• 後市觀察：景氣韌性有利獲利成長，但通膨可能推升殖利率、壓抑高評價資產。投資配置建議優先布局美國大型科技股、

美國金融股及工業資本支出受惠族群，可透過NASDAQ 100 ETF、S&P 500 ETF及美國銀行股ETF參與企業獲利成長；
固定收益則以短天期投資級債為主，以降低利率波動風險。
資料來源：Bloomberg， 2026.10.24
S&P Global PMI 走勢
資料來源：S&P Global PMI， 2026.10.24

海外資金流入美國市場，支撐美國資產吸引力
• 海外資金持續流入美國：美國財政部8月TIC資料顯示，
整體資金淨流入1,998億美元，其中民間部門淨流入
1,785億美元，為主要資金來源；外資淨買進美國長
期證券3,007億美元，遠高於美國投資人加碼海外資
產216億美元，反映全球資金在經濟成長與流動性考
量下，對美國金融市場仍維持高度的配置需求。
• 全球資金偏好美國資產：外資8月淨買入美國股票
1,319億美元，高於美國公債與公司債，顯示海外投
資人仍看好美國企業獲利與經濟基本面，雖然短期國
庫券持有量下降，部分跨境美元資金亦出現波動，但
以10月近況來看長天期公債標售仍吸引境外央行、
保險資金及大型資產管理機構參與，反映高殖利率水
準對長期資金仍具吸引力。在經濟成長維持韌性與企

業獲利支撐下，美國資產仍為全球資金配置的重要核
心市場。
• 市場看法：海外資金流入有助反映美國資產吸引力，
惟後續仍須觀察通膨、Fed政策及企業獲利表現。投
資布局建議可以美國投資等級債、美國中短天期公債
及大型AI基礎建設受惠族群為核心配置，兼顧收益、
防禦及長期成長潛力。

歷史數據顯示全球股市突破歷史新高後，後續上漲機率高
• 很多人直覺以為見高就是頂，但過去20年的統計資料顯示，全球股市在創下新高後，多數時間選擇繼續向上走。
• 勝率顯著偏高：全球股市(MSCI全球指數)創新高後持有一年，獲得正報酬的機率高達62%，三個月後的正報酬機率更達
71%。美股與台股表現最亮眼，標普500指數創高後一年上漲機率為76%，那斯達克為79%；而台灣股市表現更為驚人，創
高後三個月內上漲的機率高達82%。
• 下檔拉回風險有限：在創高後短期(一個月內)修正幅度其實相當溫和，全球多數主要市場的回徹幅度中位數僅1%～2%左右。
• 突破前高優於連續創新高：若市場在歷經至少一個月以上的整理後突破前高，其未來一年的平均報酬率高達8.4%，遠高於
連續每天創新高的3.9%。這代表市場經適度整理後的突破，具備更健康的上漲動能。

創新高後的平均遠期報酬率
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.10.22

市場寬度收窄，由少數巨頭獨撐
• 當前的市場特徵由少數巨頭帶頭衝：科技股權重較高的指數(如那斯達克、台股、韓股)，長期以來都有少數權重股拉動指數
的現象。自2024年以來，全球13個主要市場中有10個市場在創新高時，參與上漲的股票比例明顯下降。已開發市場創新高
時高於200日均線的股票比例從過去75.5%降至65.1%，新興市場更降至61.3%。這意味著指數看似風光，但多數個股並未
同步創高，當漲勢過度集中於少數大型科技巨頭時，整體市場的抗風險能力會相對減弱。
• 小結與投資建議：歷史經驗顯示，創新高本身是強勢市場的特徵，後續繼續走揚的機率遠大於立刻崩盤。然而，鑑於目前市
場寬度收窄、權重高度集中於科技股的現狀，盲目追高個股的風險已顯著增加。投資建議可以利用ETF掌握大盤趨勢，由於

指數上漲主要由龍頭股驅動，透過市值型ETF(如追蹤標普500ETF)能直接享受龍頭巨頭帶來的指數創高紅利，避免選錯個股
而賺了指數、賠了價差。此外，提防短期股市回檔，可運用定期定額分批布局或等待大盤適度拉回整理再行加碼，降低追高
風險。
創新高時不同水準的市場寬度佔比

法國政局動盪加劇財政壓力，股債同步承壓
• 法國政局動盪加劇財政壓力，股債同步承壓：法國各地抗議活動持續擴大，最初由學生發起，隨後因生活成本高漲而引發其
他群體響應。在財政空間有限、俄烏戰爭與美伊衝突推升能源價格的背景下，政府難以有效緩解民生成本壓力，法國金融市
場因此面臨股債雙跌的局面。
• 公債殖利率攀升，但系統性風險仍相對可控：市場憂心法國政府可能擴大支出以平息抗議，加劇財政可持續性疑慮。法國10
年期公債殖利率一度突破5%，法德利差亦擴大至歐債危機以來高點。不過，歐洲央行系統性壓力綜合指標（CISS）仍處於
歷史相對低檔，遠低於歐元區主權債務危機期間，顯示金融市場尚未出現廣泛的系統性壓力，短期內演變為新一輪主權債務
危機的風險仍相對有限。

日本央行本週料維持利率不變
日本央行利率走勢預估
資料來源： Bloomberg，2026.10.15 資料來源： tradingeconomics，2026.10.21
日本貿易收支
• 日本貿易逆差擴大，進口增幅高於出口：日本2026年9月貿易逆差擴大至5,366億日圓，較去年同期的2,777億日圓增加93%，
高於市場預期的1,810億日圓，連續第五個月呈現貿易赤字。出口年增16.7%至10.97兆日圓，受惠於半導體相關電子零件、
製造設備及汽車出口成長；惟進口年增18.9%至11.51兆日圓，增幅高於出口，主要受到原油、半導體電子零件及液化天然
氣進口增加帶動。其中，原油進口年增51.1%，部分反映日本在荷姆茲海峽局勢緊張下，積極分散能源採購來源。
• 日本央行本週料維持利率不變：日本央行9月將政策利率調升25個基點至1.25%，創31年來新高。考量前幾次升息對經濟的

傳導效果仍待觀察，加上貿易逆差擴大反映進口成本壓力，多數分析師預期日本央行本週將暫停升息，以評估貨幣緊縮對經
濟與通膨的影響。

中國國家統計局公布第三季GDP年增率4.5%，優於市場預期
• 中國國家統計局公布第三季GDP年增率4.5%，優於市場預期：顯示中國經濟在第二季落底後呈現溫和回升，從季度表現來看，
一、二、三季增速分別為5.0%、4.3%及4.5%，反映政策支持與外需復甦開始發揮效果，全年達成官方4.5%成長目標的機率
明顯提升，第三季經濟改善的主要動能來自出口，受惠於全球企業持續擴建AI基礎設施，及科技產品需求強勁，中國高科技
產品出口表現亮眼，尤其是積體電路、伺服器、電腦零組件，及儲存設備等產品增幅顯著，高技術製造業維持雙位數成長。
同時，中國對美國及歐盟出口維持強勁，推升貿易順差走升，為支撐經濟的重要力量。然而，內需疲弱仍是當前經濟最大隱
憂，房地產調整尚未結束，居民消費與企業投資意願保守，使經濟復甦結構呈現「外熱內冷」的特徵。

• 展望後市：在出口與科技製造業帶動下，短期成長可望維持穩定，但若內需未能有效改善，經濟成長動能恐難全面擴散，建
議聚焦在半導體、AI硬體、伺服器供應鏈，及高端製造相關產業，至於消費、房地產及傳統內需類股，仍需觀察後續刺激政
策與消費信心回升情況。
資料來源：開源證券，2026.10.22

彭博預估騰訊、阿里巴巴在中國AI競爭中勝出機率較高
• 彭博預估騰訊、阿里巴巴在中國AI競爭中勝出機率較高：彭博產業研究認為，中國AI產業未來將由大型科技平台主導，其中騰
訊、字節跳動與阿里巴巴憑藉龐大用戶基礎、數據資源、資金實力及完整生態系統，在AI競賽中具備明顯優勢，這些企業可將
AI廣泛應用於電商、社群、遊戲、金融與雲端業務，提升營運效率並強化競爭門檻，例如：阿里巴巴利用 AI 驅動電商平台上的
個人化產品推薦，而騰訊則運用 AI 優化短影音廣告並強化微信功能。騰訊自 2026 年 7 月起開始將混元 AI 模型整合至其電子
遊戲中，使遊戲角色及助理能更準確地回應玩家，然而，AI模型訓練與基礎建設投入龐大，獲利能力短期仍受壓抑，且須面對
監管、資料隱私及晶片供應等挑戰。此外，智譜、月之暗面、MiniMax及商湯等AI新創雖具技術創新能力，但缺乏規模效益、

穩定現金流及長期資本支持，未來獲利挑戰較大。
• 投資建議：看好中國AI長期發展者，可優先關注具資金、算力與應用場景優勢的大型平台企業，受惠程度可望高於多數AI新創
公司。

台灣9月外銷訂單再創新高，台股第四季有望先蹲後跳
• 台灣9月外銷訂單再創新高：9月外銷訂單1,142.1億美元，連兩個月破千億美元，再創單月新高；年增率高達62.4%，連續
20個月呈雙位數正成長。經濟部表示，AI強勁需求超乎預期，預估今年第四季接單還會優於第三季。展望10月表現，外銷
訂單將持續亮麗，估落在1,125億至1,159億美元，有望連三個月破千億美元。
• 台股第四季有望先蹲後跳：10月初以來，台股在盤中觸及歷史高點49,968點後面臨一波修正，主要反映在新一輪的財報公
布前，市場觀望AI投資是否存在泡沫化風險。短期來看，台股在季線附近出現支撐，不過在11月初的美國期中選舉落幕前，
台股維持區間震盪的可能性較高；中期來看，美國期中選後往往因政治不確定性下降，歷史統計股市上漲機率高，加上台灣

年底集團作帳及選舉行情，正向看待年底台股表現。
資料來源：經濟部統計處，兆豐銀行整理，2026.10.20。

兆豐銀行所做任何投資意見與市場分析結果，係依據資料製作當時情況進行判斷
惟可能因市場變化而變動，投資標的之價格與收益將隨時變動，亦不必然為未來績效表現 15
1. 本資料內容僅供內部同仁及客戶參考，非作為任何金融商品或服務之購買或銷售之推介，不得視為買賣有價證券或其他金融商品之要約或要約之引誘。
2. 本資料提及之經濟走勢預測不必然代表基金之績效，基金投資風險請詳閱基金公開說明書。
3. 基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良管理人之注意義務外，不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益 ，投資人申購
前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 本資料所載內容如涉及商品資訊，商品相關資訊仍以經核可之條款及文宣(DM)為主，本行網站已備有公開說明書可供查閱基金應負擔之費用及相關投資風險。投
資人申購基金係持有基金受益憑證，而非本資料提及之投資資產或標的。
5. 本資料所載內容取自本公司相信可靠之資料來源，且為特定日期之判斷，本公司不保證其即時性、精確性及完整性，亦不提供或嘗試遊說客戶做為投資之判斷依據。

資料內容邇後若有變更，本公司將不做預告或主動更新。投資人於決策時應審慎衡量本身風險，如因信賴本資料內容而做出或改變投資決策，須自行承擔結果，本
公司及本公司所屬員工，均不負任何損害賠償及其他法律上之責任。
6. 本資料內容之著作權屬兆豐銀行所有，禁止就所載資料任何形式之抄襲、引用或轉載。
7. 本資料所載部分基金涉及非投資等級債，投資人投資以非投資等級債券為訴求之基金不宜占其投資組合過高之比重。基金經金管會核准，惟不表示絕無風險。由於
非投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，故本基金可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支
付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損。部分基金不適合無法承擔相關風險之投資人。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良
管理人之注意義務外不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
8. 基金年化配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額÷除息日前一日之淨值×一年配息次數×100%。「年化配息率」為估算值。當月配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額÷除

息日前一日之淨值×100%。
9. 基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動。投資人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績
效亦不代表未來績效之保證。本資料如以基金過去績效進行模擬投資組合之報酬率時，僅為歷史資料模擬投資組合之結果，不代表本投資組合之實際報酬率及未來
績效保證，不同時間進行模擬操作，其結果亦可能不同。
10. 部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之相關費用。基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部份，可能導致原始投資金額減損。各基金由本
金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至各投信基金公司及總代理人網站查詢。
11. 屬基金資訊觀測站ESG基金專區所列之基金，有關基金之ESG資訊，投資人應於申購前詳閱基金公開說明書或投資人須知所載之基金所有特色或目標等資訊。

## Document 145: 261110_daiwa_m31

Source PDF: all report pdf/261110_daiwa_m31.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-11-10

### Page 1

Taiwan Information Technology

What’s new: Following on our results Memo, we lower our forecasts and 
TP for M31, as we factor in its still suboptimal scale and delayed 
employment bonus in 3Q26 that weigh on its trajectory of a bottom-line 
turnaround. That said, we remain comfortable with our Buy (1) call on the 
stock as we expect M31’s turnaround, kicking off from 3Q26 despite at a 
small scale, to sustain into 2027 with meaningful incremental builds, 
supported by its conversion from license to royalty revenue paying off, and 
broadened footprint into advanced technologies for price accretion and into 
diversified foundry, IDM, and fabless customers for volume accretion.

What’s the impact: Forecast changes. We lower our 2026-27E EPS for 
M31 by 14-43% to embed its still suboptimal scale and opex overhang in 
our model. Despite the cuts, its bottom-line turnaround met our expectation, 
and we expect the turnaround to sustain with meaningful incremental builds 
sequentially through 2027 to improve market confidence. We forecast its 
4Q26 top line to rebound by 23% QoQ (+12% YoY) for EPS to reach 
TWD3.31 (TWD0.35 in 3Q26), setting a good start for M31’s 2027 outlook 
and warranting a strong 213% EPS CAGR over 2026-28E.

Turnaround confirmed. While prolonged license project recognitions 
delayed M31’s turnaround, depicted in our previous Flash, we believe its 
bumpy operations over the past 2 years is finally coming to an end, 
evidenced by management’s comments of QoQ revenue growth for 4Q26 
with EPS expanding to cover accumulated losses YTD. M31 has actively 
invested over the past years enriching its IP portfolio, accumulating more 
than 2,000 license projects since 2012 (chart on p.2), and setting a good 
foundation for converting them to royalty revenue. It aims for the royalty 
revenue to outgrow license revenue in the next few quarters, after years of 
“cooking”. Meanwhile, two structural growth drivers remain intact for ASP 
and volume accretion: 1) for ASP, a growing number of high-value projects 
from advanced process nodes to enhance M31’s IP portfolio for ASP 
accretion, and 2) for volume, an increased diversification of customer base 
globally to broaden its addressable markets for volume accretion.

What we recommend: Given our forecast revisions, we lower our 12M TP 
for M31 to TWD550 (previous: TWD645), still based on our 4-quarter 
forward PER of 45x. We reaffirm our Buy (1) rating with key downside risks 
of silicon inflation and geopolitical overhang leading to global inflation and 
AI capex cut to delay M31’s royalty ramps.

How we differ: Our 2027E EPS is 52% above the consensus, likely due to 
our more positive view on royalty revenue ramp.

10 November 2026

Structural turnaround round the corner (III)

➢ Lowering our forecasts to reflect still suboptimal scale… 
➢ … but expecting enriched license projects to bear fruit soon 
➢ Reiterating our Buy (1) rating with a lower 12M TP of TWD550

Company profile 
Founded in 2011, M31 is an independent semiconductor IP provider in Taiwan, offering both foundation 
IP for specialty technology nodes and functional IP such as high-speed interface and analogue IP.

### Page 5

5

M31 Technology (6643 TT): 10 November 2026 
ESG analysis

Update Date: 11 Mar 2026 
Source: Daiwa, Company

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 2
M31's board of directors (BoD) has 7 members, including 3 independent directors, who
represent 43% of total directors, higher than the regulatory requirement of at least 20%. Its
Chairman and President are not the same person, but its President is one of the directors. M31
has the Audit Committee, Compensation Committee and Risk Control Committee under the
BoD.
Capital management 1
Although M31 is a young company (founded in 2011 and listed in 2019), its dividend payout ratio
has been over the 75% level for the past 5 years. M31 is a semiconductor IP provider without
onsite production activities, which would require high capex to do so. As such, we view its high
dividend payout ratio to be appropriate.
Related party &
transaction 1 We do not see any meaningful impact from related-party transactions.
S Supply chain management 2
M31's suppliers are required to comply with the Supplier Management Standards, which was set
by M31 in 2013. In addition, M31 has periodical evaluations for its suppliers and onsite audits to
ensure the quality and implementation of Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR), as well as
Environmental Protection and Safety and Integrity Policies.
E Water & wastewater management 1 M31 is a semiconductor IP provider without physical products, and thus no production activity to affect the environment.
E
Waste & hazardous
materials
management
1
The company is a semiconductor IP provider without physical products, and thus no production
activity to affect the environment. It still adopts some measures to reduce waste from its
operations, such as recycling and using electronic files to replace paper documents as much as
possible to reduce the use of paper.
E GHG emissions 1 M31 is a semiconductor IP provider without physical products, and thus no production activity to affect the environment.
Note: Management score represents a company's ability to manage/benefit from certain ESG topics. The scores range from 1 to 3, with 1 being the strongest.

Update
ASMedia Technology Inc  | Asia Pacific
Inventory correction extends
into 1Q27
Inventory adjustment extends into 1Q27. Management sees improving
motherboard sell-through, but expects customer destocking to constrain shipments
in 4Q26–1Q27. AM4 exceeded 70% of chipset shipments in 3Q and should remain
dominant in 4Q, weighing on ASPs. Management indicated GM of around 50% in
4Q26–1Q27 as foundry and backend costs rise; alternative packaging/testing sources

should begin helping from 2Q27. October's stronger proprietary mix provided some
relief, but 2027 revenue guidance remains unfinalized, with 20% growth now
considered aggressive.
Recovery likely depends on execution from 2H27. ASMedia signed a new SOW
with the customer and currently sees itself as the sole supplier. Management hopes
shipments will start in 2Q27, with meaningful platform volume from 3Q27; ASPs
would be at least 50–60% above AM4. PCIe switches contributed 6–8% of revenue

after Gen4 yield and thermal issues delayed design-ins, versus a double-digit mix
target for 2027. USB4 is expected to more than double from 2026's depressed base,
although robotics remains small. New products can support recovery, but higher
development spending limits near-term operating leverage.
Morgan Stanley Taiwan Limited+
Daniel Yen, CFA
Equity Analyst

Valuation Methodology and Risks
ASMedia Technology Inc (5269.TW)
Base case, residual income model. Key assumptions: cost of equity of 9.8% (beta 1.3, risk-free
rate 2% and risk premium 6%), medium-term growth rate of 8.0%, payout ratio of 75% and
terminal growth rate of 5.2%.
Risks to Upside
n AMD gains market share thanks to better-than-expected performance.
n In-house designed products grow faster than expected.
n US-China tensions improve and US tariffs alleviate.

Risks to Downside
n AMD cannot get substrate supply, causing earnings downside for ASMedia.
n Intel adopts TSMC's technologies more aggressively to compete with AMD.
n Spec migration on USB 4 is slower.

STOCK RATINGS
Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy,
Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell.  Investors should carefully read the definitions of all
ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan

Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone.  In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice.  An investor's decision
to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  1

2026/11/09 投資速報

電子–半導體 
世芯-KY (3661.TT) 
破除市場謠言，27 年獲利上修

強力買進(調升評等) 
出刊緣由：財報公佈

加權指數報酬率 -20.3 -10.6 -3.5 42.3 
個股股價報酬率 -32.3 -22.1 -37.5 -22.3

營收走勢圖

研究員/吳大任 TERRY.WU@uni-psg.com

研究部調升世芯-KY 的投資建議為強力買進，目標價上修至 6850
元，基於以下五點理由：(1)3Q26 雖受到供應鏈緊張影響，營收仍
持續創高，(2)4Q26 營收將持續攀升，(3)27 年 CSP 3 代晶片貢獻
營收幅度上修，(4)新增 AI 晶片客戶，有望在 27 年下半年貢獻約
10 億美元營收，(5)CSP 4 代晶片更加確立，28 年營收更高層級的
營收，且費用控管得宜，OPM 純度持續增加。綜合以上，維持 26 年
EPS 預估為 120.31 元(原估120.3 元，+65.5%YOY)，上修 27 年 EPS
預估為 198.3 元(原估 178.99 元，+64.8%YOY)，及預估 28 年 EPS
為 341.95 元(+72.5%YOY)。雖然 1H26 營運動能表現不佳，但最差
狀況確實已過，目前供應鏈因素逐步排除，且在 27 年 CSP 客戶的
訂單配比增加，且已經證明世芯為北美 CSP 客戶唯一後段設計服務
供應商的角色，新增客戶產品也將在 27 年貢獻，先前股價修正幅
度較大，故上修建議至強力買進，目標價上修至 6850 元(27 年
EPS*35XPER OR 28 年EPS*20XPER)。

重點摘要：

1. 3Q26 受到供應鏈影響，惟營收仍維持高檔：  
世芯-KY 3Q26 營收為 261.9 億元(原估 284.5 億元)，成長
242.8%QOQ，成長 298.7%YOY，小幅不如原先預期主要係因
載板及封裝產能稀缺等負面影響，如產能有爭取夠將有望
增加約 1 億美元之營收，就與先前預估相當。毛利率下滑
至 17.95%，與原先預估 17.5%相差不大，主要是部分 NRE
有遞延認列影響。費用 10.7 億元，稍微高於於預估，不過
整體稅後淨利仍達到 32.51 億元(原估 33.09 億元)，成長
98.5%QOQ，成長 145%YOY，EPS 37.67 元(原估 39.28 元)，
差異來自於營收規模。

2. 4Q26 營收將續創新高： 
展望 4Q26，雖然在法說會上公司未提及財測數字預估，不
過 CSP 客戶 3nm 案件持續放量，先前有供應端材料不順的
影響，與供應商及客戶偕同持續排除下，推估營收有望續
創新高。故研究部維持 4Q26 營收預估為 334.5 億元，成長
27.7%QOQ，成長 607.6%YOY，毛利率與先前預估相當的
17.5%，稅後淨利預估為 40.66 億元(原估 39.1 億元)，成
長 25.1%QOQ，成長 174.2%YOY，EPS 47.11 元(原估 45.35
元)。

### Page 2

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  2

3. CSP 3 代晶片推估 26 年貢獻 15 億美金，27 年確定上修 
法說會雖然因法規及與客戶 NDA 的關係，皆無提到 CSP 客
戶三代晶片能夠貢獻的營收幅度，不過從營收可以看出，
先前規劃的投片持續順利產出，26 年維持貢獻 15 億美元
營收不變。展望 27 年，先前預估較為保守，主要係因 3nm
產能稀缺，但此次法說會公司表示，在研發上持續配合客
戶，而客戶依賴度提升狀況下，27 年的訂單配比有所提升，
產能也有所確保，故上修成長幅度至 100%YOY 以上，先前
僅預估 60-70%YOY 左右。

4. CSP 4 代晶片訂單更加確立，且新增另一客戶： 
另外在 4 代晶片方面，法說會當中有提到 2nm 案件將在
4Q26 TAPE OUT，且再次宣告也只有一家後段設計服務供
應商，包含 IO DIE 及連結部分，且公司也扮演整合晶片的
角色，以時間點來看，將有望在 4Q27 做小規模的量產，且
僅有 ANNAPURNA 及世芯有在台積電投片該晶片的權力。 
此次在法說會有提到，公司新增另外一個大客戶，公司未
透露細節，但非 GOOGLE，客戶為美國客戶，晶片就是與 AI
相關(未指出是推論還是訓練)，且該晶片在 27 年下半年
會貢獻營收，推估營收規模將達到 10 億美元。而世芯與該
客戶的生意將具有延續性。 
故綜合以上，世芯對於 28 年營收規模期待將有更大的跳
升，且 OPM 將會往 HIGH TEENS 邁進。

5. 26年約略維持預估，27年預估上修，28年粗估高度成長： 
3Q26世芯財報約略符合預期，雖然有供應鏈相關問題，不
過4Q26營收仍將續創新高，故維持26年營收預估為714.6
億元(原估737億元)，EPS 120.31元(原估120.3元)。展望
27年，CSP客戶三代晶片貢獻全年，加上爭取到的投片及訂
單配比上修，另外新增客戶也將貢獻營收，故上修營收為
1529.3億元(原估1335億元)，EPS上修至198.3元(原估
178.99元)。28年在CSP 4代晶片開始貢獻下，研究部粗估
1Q28小幅量產，2Q28開始大幅放量，推估28年營收將達到
2593億元，成長72.5%YOY，EPS預估再跳升至341.95元。

附錄一 ： 評 等 之 標 準 
評等 定義 
強力買進    預估未來 6 個月內的絕對報酬超過 25%以上 
買進 預估未來 6 個月內的絕對報酬 10%以上 
中立 預估未來 6 個月內的絕對報酬介於 10～-10% 
降低持股 預估未來 6 個月內的絕對報酬介於(-10%)以下 
未評等 沒有足夠的基本資料判斷該公司評等

資 標 的 之 要 約 、 宣 傳 或 引 誘 等 事 項 。 對 於 本 投 資 報 告 所 討 論 或 建 議 之 任 何 證 券 、 投
資 標 的 ， 或 文 中 所 討 論 或 建 議 之 投 資 策 略 ， 投 資 人 應 就 其 是 否 適 合 本 身 財 務 狀 況 與

## Document 288: [402340] SK스퀘어(402340) SK하이닉스 최대주주SK스퀘어(402340) SK하이닉스 최대주주SK하이닉스 최대주주 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[402340] SK스퀘어(402340) SK하이닉스 최대주주SK스퀘어(402340) SK하이닉스 최대주주SK하이닉스 최대주주 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-29
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

SK 스퀘어 (402340/KS) 
SK 하이닉스 최대주주 
SK 하이닉스 4 분기도 배당 주당 20,000 원 이상  
SK 하이닉스는 전일 주당 20,000 원(실적반영: 10,000 원, 키옥시아 특별배당: 
10,000원)의 3분기 배당을 발표하였다. 키옥시아 특별배당은 3분기 배당과 26년 회
계연도 결산배당으로 나누어 지급예정이다. 이에 SK 스퀘어는 2조 9,220억원(1분기
와 2분기 배당수입은 각 548억원)의 배당수입을 수취하게 된다. SK하이닉스는 26년 
전체 현금 배당 규모는 30조원 이상을 발표했는데, 3분기까지 지급하는 배당금은 14.6
조원이기 때문에 4분기에도 최소한 주당 20.000원 이상의 배당이 예상된다. 이 경우 
SK 스퀘어의 26 년 배당수입은 6 조원에 달할 것으로 예상한다. SK 스퀘어는 ‘26 년
~’28년 경상배당수입의 30% 이상을 주주에게 환원하는 주주환원정책을 보유하고 있
어 SK스퀘어의 현 시가총액 대비 27년 주주환원율은 최소 1.5% 이상 될 전망이다.

SK 하이닉스 자사주 취득·소각으로 높아질 지배력 
SK하이닉스는 20조원의 자사주 매입을 발표하였다. 이 중 10조원은 임직원 보상목적
이며, 10조원은 주주환원 목적이다. 26년 총 60조원 이상의 자사주 취득·소각 예정(8
월 결의분 40 조원 포함)으로 임직원 보상 목적의 자사주 매입은 제외되기 때문에 26
년 실적 확정 후 추가적으로 10조원 이상의 자사주 취득이 예상된다. 기 취득한 자사주 
40조원(3.3%)는 11월 5일 소각 예정으로 소각 이후 SK스퀘어의 SK하이닉스 지분
율은 20.5%(2 분기말 기준)에서 21.2%로 상승하게 된다. 이번 발표한 10 조원
(1.0%) 자사주 소각시 SK 스퀘어의 지분율은 21.4%이 되며, 추가로 10 조원을 추가 
소각한다면 21.7%까지 지분율이 상승하게 된다.

SK 하이닉스 대비 높은 베타 
SK스퀘어에 대한 투자의견 매수를 유지한다. SK스퀘어 NAV의 98%가량을 차지하
는 SK하이닉스의 현금배당 증가로 SK스퀘어의 주주환원 재원이 확대되고, SK하이닉
스의 자사주 취득·소각으로 지배력이 강화되기 때문이다. SK하이닉스는 전일 컨퍼런스 
콜을 통해 29년까지 D램과 HBM 물량이 사실상 모두 솔드아웃된 상황이라 밝히며 주
가 반등 가능성이 높은데, SK 스퀘어 베타(1.8)는 SK 하이닉스(1.7) 대비 높기 때문에 
SK 하이닉스 주가 반등에 따른 SK 스퀘어의 동반 주가 상승을 기대할 수 있다. 현재 
SK스퀘어의 NAV 대비 할인율은 46.5%이며, SK하이닉스가 자사주 60조원 소각시 
할인율은 49.4%까지 상승하게 된다.

2026-10-29

지주회사/통신서비스/ESG. 최관순 / ks1.choi@sks.co.kr / 3773-8812

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
As expected, the RBA Monetary Policy Board (MPB) lifted
the cash rate 25bps to 4.85% at its November meeting.
This follows the increase at the previous (late-September)
meeting and is a marked turnaround from the outlook back
in August. The post-meeting media statement cited higher

global energy prices resulting from the Middle East conflict,
tighter domestic capacity pressures and an increase in
inflation expectations for the decision.
However, the decision was a split vote, 6 votes to 3, a
possibility we have flagged for some time. In the post-meeting
media conference, Governor Bullock explained that the
dissenting minority put more weight on the downside risks and
did not see signs of wages growth picking up as much as the

staff forecasts implied. Those members were reportedly more
confident that slower growth would prevent higher inflation
expectations becoming entrenched.
Financial conditions are restrictive and this is expected to
bring inflation down over time. Because the RBA assesses the
risks to the inflation outlook to still be skewed to the upside,
the outlook for coming meetings will still be for a decision
between holding and hiking. But we note that the post-meeting

statement omitted September’s language “including increasing
the cash rate target further if needed” from the commitment to
do what is necessary to get inflation down.
In the post-meeting media conference, the Governor
commented that after five hikes, this is probably a good time
to “take stock”. Together with the dissents, we read this as
confirming our current view that the MPB will keep rates
on hold in December. There is also considerable data flow

between now and then, which could go either way, including
the Q3 national accounts.
Similarly, a lot could happen between now and February, but
we see it as the next most likely timing for a hike, should one
eventuate. We still see that as a risk rather than a base case.
Revised forecasts published in the accompanying Statement
on Monetary Policy (SMP) show underlying inflation lingering
around current rates for longer. This contrasts with the
previous SMP forecasts in August, which showed trimmed

mean inflation returning to the 2–3%yr target range by late
2027 and a little below the 2.5%yr target midpoint in 2028.
We note, however, that the conditioning assumptions on oil
prices, which are based on futures prices, were at a local peak
at the time the forecasts were finalised.
RBA hikes cash rate to new high of 4.85%, a December pause likely
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
The RBA’s GDP growth forecasts for 2027 and 2028 were
both revised down –0.3%pts to 1.3% for 2027 and 1.5% in

2028, despite an upward revision to the near term. This is
well below even the RBA’s downbeat view of potential output
growth. Within the GDP aggregate, recent misses and leading
indicators have required upward revisions to the near-term
outlooks for business and dwelling investment, and downside
revisions to public demand. These revisions were offset further
out with downward revisions to the 2028 outlook for business
and dwelling investment and an upward revision to public

demand. Household consumption forecasts were revised down
across most of the profile.
With the unemployment rate already at 4.7% in August and
September – a rate the RBA did not forecast until end-2027 in
its August SMP forecasts – the RBA has lifted its unemployment
forecast to show a peak of 5.1% in at end-2028, the end of the
forecast horizon. This largely reflects stronger labour supply,
with forecasts for employment growth essentially unchanged.

We have repeatedly flagged that cost-of-living pressures are
likely to keep people in the labour force and thus boost the
participation rate. The RBA had previously not factored this
adequately into its forecasts but acknowledged in the SMP
that this mechanism had kept participation higher than it had
expected at the time of the August SMP.
However, because inflation surprised to the upside recently,
this additional labour supply has had no bearing on the

RBA’s assessment of capacity pressures. The RBA assesses
the domestic economy to still be a little tight, and tighter
than previously judged. The RBA’s framework for assessing
the degree of spare capacity or tightness in the economy
necessarily translates an upside surprise on inflation (or unit
labour costs) into a conclusion that the economy must be
tighter than previously judged. Indeed, the RBA’s estimate
of the NAIRU (unemployment rate consistent with inflation

stable at target) has been revised up to 4.75%.
The RBA highlighted that elevated inflation expectations may
be contributing to higher realised inflation. Consumer inflation
expectations have started to lift again but remain below where
they peaked earlier this year. However, the RBA appears
“RBA lifts cash rate 25bps to 4.85% in split
vote, citing “broadly based” domestic inflation
pressures, Middle East conflict and AI boom."

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
to be putting more attention on the inflation expectations
of firms and the frequency of mentions of pricing in their
liaison program. This is putting a lot of weight on proprietary
information that is not clearly validated by other, longer-
running data sources.

The AI boom is heavily cited in the SMP and other post-
meeting communication. The RBA has lifted its forecasts
for major trading partners, especially high-income Asian
economies that are the centre of high-tech manufacturing.
Demand for AI-related goods is also cited as a driver of both
global and domestic inflation. And although the RBA has
revised up its near-term forecasts for business investment
on the back of strong data centre activity, it is working on

the assumption that Australia does not become ‘a significant
regional hub for data centres’. Nor does it allow for any boost
to productivity trends from the technology, even in 2028. This
is a very different view of the risks from AI than the RBNZ
expressed last week. For the RBA, it is all about inflation, a
little bit about pressure on domestic construction resources,
and nothing about either an ugly unwind of the current
financing boom or a future productivity dividend.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: whiplash
total to $11.1bn. This is another clear marker of the scale of
Australia’s data-centre investment boom, which should feature
prominently in both business investment and imports in the Q3
National Accounts.
Offshore, the world has been focused on the US mid-term

elections. In the House, Senate and a series of State Governor
races, the Democrats experienced resounding victory. Come
2027, the Democrats will have a comfortable majority in both
houses of Congress and are expected to make use of this
strength to respond to voter concerns, particularly over cost-
of-living pressures. The election result also limits President
Trump’s ability to fund an extended military action against
Iran. Notably, both immediately before and after the election,

President Trump and the administration have exhibited a greater
interest in furthering negotiations with Iran. It is therefore not
a surprise that Brent oil has backed off its most recent highs
around USD118 last Friday, although the scale of the drop to
near USD93 yesterday is arguably too optimistic. Brent has since
traded back up to above USD99, still 16% below Friday’s peak.
While the initial reaction to the US election result was a
robust broad-based rally in major equity indexes, concerns

over the potential for tighter regulation of AI and the related
infrastructure buildout jolted US equities lower overnight – the
Nasdaq falling 5.5% and S&P500 4.4%, both a multiple of
the prior session’s gains. That said, US term interest rates and
FOMC rate expectations have also been scaled back; so, while
tech may come under greater scrutiny, a foundation for better
outcomes across the rest of the US economy may become
evident in time. US data out this week continued to suggest

their economy is in robust health overall. And, if tensions in
the Middle East abate, the FOMC’s concerns over US inflation
should subside. Gaining enough comfort for the FOMC to not
hike in December is a high bar, but the probability of further
hikes in 2027 should continue to fall.
Turning then to the UK, the Bank of England raised Bank Rate
by 25bps to 4.00% at their November meeting as expected.
On the Bank's baseline expectation, UK inflation is forecast to

rise from 3.5%yr at September to 5.00% by mid-2027. Activity
data has also surprised to the upside in recent months, though
"there remains limited evidence of knock-on effects on other
prices and wages".
In Australia, the RBA delivered another 25bp rate increase
at its November meeting, taking the cash rate to 4.85%. The
move was widely expected, reflecting the Board’s continued
concern around inflation following the recent run higher in
global energy prices, firmer domestic capacity pressures and

signs that inflation expectations have edged up. Inflation
is now not expected to return to the mid-point of the
target band until mid-2028. This is despite weaker growth
expectations and the unemployment rate trending up to
5.1% by end-2028 on the RBA’s forecasts.
Importantly, though, the decision was not unanimous, with
three members preferring to remain on hold given downside
risks to activity and the restraining influence of slower growth.
Governor Bullock’s suggestion that it was time to “take stock”

after five rate increases also struck a less hawkish note,
while the statement dropped September’s explicit reference
to raising rates further “if needed”. In our view, February
represents the next plausible opportunity for another hike, but
we see this only as a risk.
Policy’s effect on housing is becoming increasingly visible.
Cotality home prices fell a further 1.2% in October to now
be 6.7% below March’s peak. The cumulative price decline
is approaching the scale of previous housing corrections but

appears to have regained momentum after September’s rate
increase. Dwelling approvals saw a 5.1% headline gain in
September, but this was driven by volatile unit approvals while
detached house approvals fell sharply, suggesting higher rates
and the established-market downturn are beginning to weigh
on the construction pipeline.
Official data on nominal household spending meanwhile
rose 0.6% in September and 2.1% for Q3; however, much of
the recent acceleration looks to be price-driven, particularly

for fuel. Real spending volumes expanded by a much more
modest (but still solid) 0.7% in Q3.
The Westpac-DataX Consumer Panel provides a granular look at
income, spending and saving flows. The latest update suggests
that, beneath the resilient headline story, households are
increasingly being squeezed, particularly those with low savings
buffers. With another rate increase now delivered, housing
wealth declining and sentiment weak, Q3 increasingly looks like

the high-water mark for growth in consumer spending.
Lastly for Australia, in September the goods trade surplus
widened sharply to $2.8bn, with non-monetary gold trade
yet again adding considerable noise month-to-month. The
most notable development was another exceptionally strong
result for data-processing equipment imports, taking the Q3
Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics
Ryan Wells, Economist

policy this week, in part due to price pressures
emanating from the Middle East."

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
In the press conference, Governor Bailey recognised the
nascent improvement in energy price pressures over the
past 48 hours and the effectiveness of monetary policy but
also conveyed that "Unless progress towards resolving the
conflict is made, or there is other news which softens the risk

to inflationary pressures, a further increase in Bank Rate may
be warranted". Governor Bailey's tone and comments made in
the minutes by other Committee members did not suggest this
was a decision that needs to be made in December, but rather
a risk to be judged as additional information is received. This
points to further tightening being more likely in early 2027
than before, and only if conditions / risks warrant.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
No deals are done
This week’s developments underscore our message that
a rate hike at the RBNZ’s December meeting is far from a
done deal. New Zealand’s labour market remains softer
than expected, and that will be noted by those on the

policy committee who question the extent of second-round
inflation pressures. The rout in the US mid-term elections
has also taken significant heat out of oil prices for now,
although it’s unclear whether that situation will hold through
to next month. Our view on the endpoint for this OCR cycle
remains unchanged, but there’s a growing risk that the RBNZ
could slow the pace of tightening in the new year.
The September quarter labour market surveys pointed to

ongoing softness in the jobs market. The unemployment
rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7%, continuing a four-year run
of increases, and reaching its highest level since 2015.
Employment was softer than expected, and wage growth
remains subdued despite increasing cost-of-living pressures.
The Household Labour Force Survey (HLFS) showed a surprise
0.1% fall in employment in the September quarter, after a
downwardly revised 0.3% increase in the June quarter. That

went against the Monthly Employment Indicator (MEI) measure
of filled jobs, which had suggested some upward momentum
emerging in the jobs market (and notably, the HLFS measure
had outpaced the MEI for the previous nine straight quarters).
The fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a fall in
the labour force participation rate from 70.6% to 70.4%.
These co-movements essentially reversed the surprisingly
large increases that we saw in the June quarter, which we had

suggested at the time were more likely due to sampling error
in the survey rather than a genuine surge.
We generally recommend focusing on the unemployment rate,
which is less vulnerable to sampling errors – and the message
here is one of softness. Our best interpretation is that, to the
extent that jobs are growing, it’s not fast enough to absorb the
growth in the population (which itself is a fairly low bar, as net
migration remains at a low ebb).

Even with the economy returning to growth over the last
year or so, hiring activity has remained weak. This may be
an ongoing legacy of employment trends in previous years.
Businesses have told us that they felt burned by the severe
skill shortages that developed over 2021-23, and looked to
hold on to workers to the extent they could during the 2024-
25 downturn. The flipside of this is that they may now find
themselves overstaffed even as activity picks up again.

This shows up most strikingly in labour force transition rates.
The rise in unemployment over the last few years has not
been driven by an increase in job losses, but by accumulation
of people who, once out of work, are finding it increasingly
difficult to get back in again. That also extends to young
people trying to enter the workforce for the first time – youth
unemployment of around 25% is the highest in the history of
the survey going back to 1986.

The Labour Cost Index (LCI) rose by 0.5% in the September
quarter, in line with our expectations overall but a bit weaker
in the details – private sector wages were up by 0.5%, while
there was a surprisingly large 0.7% increase in public sector
wages. There was a notable jump in the number of employers
citing cost-of-living adjustments as a reason for increasing pay
rates – although the size of those increases varied a great deal,
with many substantially less than the headline inflation rate.

Potential RBNZ implications
The labour market data was unequivocally weaker than
expected by either us or the RBNZ. While likely not a
gamechanger for the cycle overall, it will add ammunition to the
arguments of the doves on the Monetary Policy Committee that
a pause in interest rate rises will make sense soon – perhaps in
December or early next year. It’s likely the case that interest rate
markets were over-pricing the odds of a hike at the 9 December

MPS, so the subsequent pullback in pricing makes sense.
The message from the RBNZ in the October meeting was to
be careful about pricing in "done deals", and that the outlook
Michael Gordon
Senior Economist

### Page 7

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
7
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
will be data-dependent. As we noted at the time, alongside
measures of inflation expectations, the key domestic data
to watch ahead of the RBNZ’s 9 December meeting will
be indicators of what is happening to excess capacity (the
output gap), as this will be a key determinant of the Bank’s

assessment of the outlook for inflation over the coming year.
The RBNZ looks at a broad suite of indicators of labour market
slack, although their research found that only four of them
seem to have predictive power: the unemployment rate,
the vacancy to unemployment ratio, the QSBO measure of
labour as the main limiting factor to growth, and the rate of
transition between jobs. The first three of these all pointed
to a softening in the September quarter. (The fourth one is

calculated from income tax data, so it’s not publicly available
and even the RBNZ receives it only with a lag.)
In the eyes of the dovish MPC members, the softness in
the labour market indicators will likely push in the direction
of having greater comfort with the medium-term inflation
outlook. The outcome of the election, with the possibility of a
return to a dual inflation/employment mandate, could increase
the prominence of these factors. This likely won’t be an explicit

factor in the RBNZ’s reasoning at its December review, but it
may be in the minds of individual MPC members.
That said, it’s important to remember that in the RBNZ’s
forecasting framework, the GDP figures are the main
determinant of excess capacity in the economy. The most
recent GDP data tells us that the output gap probably
narrowed a bit through the June quarter, mainly reflecting
historical revisions. In light of the latest labour market figures,

there may be some downside risk to the outlook for Q3 GDP –
but there are conflicting indicators there, so it’s not that clear.
At last month’s surprise on-hold OCR decision, Governor
Breman lamented that there was no protocol for the RBNZ
to be able to signal how it was reading the economic data –
in particular the softer than expected Q3 CPI print – ahead
of the policy meeting. In that light, it will be interesting to
see whether they use some already-scheduled speaking

engagements to signal what they make of the labour market
data. Top of mind is Chief Economist Conway's 10 November
speech to the UBS Australasia Conference (pointedly titled
“How is the NZ economy tracking”). Governor Breman is also
speaking to the Institute of Directors on 19 November.
Our view remains that the December OCR hike is more likely
than not. But there will be a clear debate to be had between
the MPC hawks and doves, and it may again come down to a

vote. We still expect a 4% peak in the OCR for this cycle, but
the time taken to get there could be more flexible. Takes along
the lines of "3% is the terminal rate" seem rather hasty on the
basis of a single data release.
Aside from how the Committee interprets the labour market
data, the key considerations in December will be: the RBNZ’s
surveys of inflation expectations by households and businesses;
whether the US mid-term elections lead to a sustained de-

escalation in the Middle East and a pullback in oil prices; and
whether developments in the AI space exacerbate some MPC
members’ concerns about the global economic environment.
And then of course there’s the result of the General Election,
with voting closing on Saturday evening. The actual formation
of a government is likely to take weeks of negotiation, so the
outcome may not be known by the 9 December MPS but at least
the range of possibilities should be clearer. We’ll publish our

initial thoughts on the election results early on Monday morning.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
NZ: Oct Retail Card Spending (%mth)
Nov 12, Last: –0.4, Westpac f/c: 1.4
We’re forecasting a 1.4% rise in retail spending in October.
While that would be a solid increase, it’s in large part due to
the 11% rise in petrol prices over the past month. Stripping out

fuel spending, we’re forecasting a 0.6% rise in core spending
categories. Core spending has been very choppy recently,
and the increase we’re assuming follows falls in the past few
months. A result in line with our expectations would leave the
trend in core spending relatively flat since the start of this year
with the rise in fuel prices weighing on spending in other areas.
AUS: Nov Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment (index)
Nov 10, Last: 84.4
Consumer sentiment fell 4.7% to 80.4 in Oct, returning to the

deeply pessimistic levels seen earlier in the year. Responses
over the course of the survey week showed a very sharp
deterioration after the RBA rate hike.
The Nov survey is in the field over the week ended Nov 7.
Interest rates will again factor with the RBA raising the cash
rate by another 25bps to 4.85%, an 18yr high. Average pump
prices also pushed higher, the national average just under
$2.40/litre, while the housing market downturn has continued

to broaden and deepen. The US mid-term election results may
have some effects on sentiment locally as well.
AUS: Q3 Housing Finance Approvals (%qtr)
Nov 11 Last: –5.2, Westpac f/c: –10
Market f/c: –8.5, Range: –15 to –5
The total value of housing finance approvals fell The value of
new dwelling finance approvals excl. refinance fell 5.2%qtr,
the largest quarterly drop in three years. Annual growth eased
to 7.3%yr. The weakness was accounted for mainly by lower

lending volumes: loan numbers were down 4.0%qtr (–0.6%yr),
while average loan size also contributed negatively.
Q3 is expected to see an even bigger decline with the full
impact of interest rate rises and tax policy changes showing
through. The value of both loan enquiries and turnover fell by
around 10% in the quarter. We expect total finance approvals
to show a 10% drop as well, with investor loan approvals likely
to be down 16%qtr and owner occupier approvals down 6%qtr.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
US: Oct CPI (%mth)
Nov 11, Headline, Last: 0.6, Market f/c: 0.3, Westpac f/c: 0.4
US consumer prices rose by 0.6% in September, although
half of the gain was due to energy inflation, with core prices
instead up 0.3%. Over the year, prices were reported to have

risen 3.7%yr overall and 2.6%yr excluding food and energy.
Constructively, ex food, energy and shelter, annual inflation
met the FOMC’s medium-term target of 2.0%yr.
Come October, energy price pressures are set to recede
given the pre-election run higher in oil prices came late in the
month. A softer print for shelter is also likely to moderate core
momentum. We see a 0.4% gain for the headline measure and
a 0.2% result for core inflation as most probable. Looking to

2027, disinflation is expected to continue at a measured pace
towards the medium-term target.

## Document 334: Cliff Notes_ cost of living front and centre (2026-11-16)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cliff Notes_ cost of living front and centre (2026-11-16).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-16
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: cost of living front and centre
By Ryan Wells, Elliot Clarke & Illiana Jain
09:49 November 13 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
In Australia, the Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment Index fell 2.7% to 78.2 in November, its weakest
reading since late-2022 and a level historically associated with periods of severe economic stress.
Renewed cost-of-living and interest rate pressures were front and centre following another lift in

inflation, including sharply higher petrol prices, and the RBA’s November rate increase. Mortgage
holders are feeling the squeeze most acutely, with sentiment across this group falling to a record low.

### Page 2

Consumers have become noticeably more downbeat on the near-term economic outlook – the relevant
sub-index dropping 10.2% to be 22% below its long-run average. Job-loss fears also continue to edge
up.
Businesses are similarly troubled. The latest NAB business survey showed confidence slipped further
to –15 in October as conditions held near their weakest level since the pandemic. Of concern for the
outlook, forward orders fell to –12, also the weakest read since the pandemic, and capital expenditure

intentions deteriorated. Purchase costs continue to grow materially faster than selling prices, further
compressing already tight margins.
Housing provides a clear example of how caution and tighter financial conditions can jolt activity. The
value of new dwelling finance approvals plunged 15% in Q3, the largest quarterly decline in the series’
history, taking approvals 25% lower year-to-date. Investor lending bore the brunt of the adjustment,
falling 30% over the quarter as higher rates combined with the housing tax changes announced in May.

Owner-occupier lending also weakened, however, with first-home buyers proving particularly sensitive
to the deterioration in affordability and market conditions.
Taken together, the data points to a further loss of momentum across the domestic economy,
consistent with Westpac’s expectation for subdued growth and concern over downside activity risks.
Meanwhile, in the US, October’s headline CPI met our expectations but at 0.4% was a touch above the

consensus view. Energy and food both contributed meaningfully to the result, but core inflation was
also marginally stronger than forecast, +0.3%. Core goods inflation has accelerated over the past four
months to average a gain of 0.2% as businesses look to pass tariffs, tech and energy input cost
inflation through. Shelter meanwhile continued to rise at an above-average pace, gaining 0.3% in
October.
The market impact of the October CPI report was accentuated by hawkish remarks from Fed Governor

Waller prior to the release. He expressed confidence in economic activity and the labour market while
showing significant concern over the strength and persistence of inflation, focusing on the passthrough
of energy and technology input inflation. His conclusion for policy was clear: "if, as I expect, the
monthly rate of inflation improves in October, I am leaning toward supporting an increase in our policy
rate of 25 basis points at the FOMC's December meeting"; but "if the October CPI inflation data out

later today are similar to the past two monthly readings—with core CPI inflation around 0.3 percent or
even higher—then a stronger policy move may be needed".
Regional Fed Presidents Goolsbee, Logan and Hammack also kept the focus on inflation risks and
stated or implied the FOMC needs to act. Market participants have responded to this week’s updates,
pricing a 25bp hike in for the December and January meetings, and a cumulative 3.5 hikes by
September. We believe this pricing overstates the need for policy tightening given core inflation ex

shelter remains relatively benign at 2.4% on a six-month annualised basis and the labour market is
balanced; but it will take time for price concerns to subside.
While we agree that the US economy is strong in aggregate, US households are under pressure from
the cost of living, and their investment decision making is constrained by borrowing costs. Highlighting

### Page 3

Related articles
this, US existing home sales fell 5.5% in October after a downwardly revised 1.0% gain in September,
leaving the level of sales below the cycle lows of 2009 and 2010. Our November Market Outlook will
provide a full update on our expectations for the US and the world when released on Westpac IQ this
afternoon.

## Document 349: Double Feature_ The Spy Who Loved Me & To China With Love

Date: 2026-11-13
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: Double Feature: The Spy Who Loved Me & To China With Love
Date:    8 hours ago
Double Feature: The Spy Who Loved Me & To China With Love

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Double Feature: The Spy Who
Loved Me & To China With Love
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 12 Nov 2026
View in browser
Photo by Vows on the Move / Unsplash

I. Jensen Versus The AI Trade
Charlie Munger famously warned that there are three ways smart men can
go broke: "Liquor, ladies, and leverage." NVIDIA co-founder and now
former CEO Jensen Huang has run into trouble with the second of
Munger's three L's. He won't go broke, but he is out of a good job.
On Thursday, NVIDIA announced that Huang had resigned as CEO after
33 years at the helm of the company he co-founded in 1993. His

### Page 2

departure followed revelations of extramarital affairs, including a reported
relationship with a Chinese businesswoman suspected by US intelligence
officials of spying for Beijing. Her Singapore-based company, Megaspeed,
allegedly purchased NVIDIA's high-powered GPU chips and re-exported
them to China, violating US export restrictions. The longtime CFO Colette
Kress was appointed interim CEO while the board searches for a
successor.
Huang's departure rattled the AI trade, with NVIDIA shares plunging 4.3%,

sending its forward P/E to 11.7 (charts). We promised you buying
opportunities after the midterm elections. There were a bunch last
Thursday and a few more today.

### Page 3

Competitors AMD and Broadcom advanced 5.9% and 5.5%, respectively,
on expectations of market-share gains (chart)
As a result, the Nasdaq Composite held up relatively well, falling just 0.4%
today (chart).
Neoclouds also tumbled, with CoreWeave down 6.7% and Nebius down
16.8%, amid concerns about NVIDIA-backed financing (chart). Nebius
was additionally pressured by disappointing revenue guidance and rising
capital spending.

### Page 4

Despite the latest AI jitters, the two-year Treasury yield rose 15bps to
4.87% this evening, remaining well above the federal funds rate and
signaling that further Fed tightening is warranted (chart). The 10-year
Treasury yield is back above 5.00% at 5.13% this evening.
Yields rose on higher oil prices, with Brent crude up 3.1% to $95.66 this
evening, after President Donald Trump dismissed the need for an Iran
deal, and on hawkish Fed commentary following October's hot CPI report

(chart).

### Page 5

Markets now price a 100% probability of a December Fed rate hike:
84.7% for 25bps and 15.3% for 50bps. Futures imply more than 3 rate
hikes over the next 12 months (chart).
The combination of higher Treasury yields, driven by rising oil prices and
hawkish Fed commentary, and renewed AI jitters weighed on the broader
stock market. The S&P 500 fell 0.4%, its equal-weighted counterpart
declined 0.9%, and the Russell 2000 lost 1.7% (charts).

### Page 6

II. Nvidia: The Bear Case
We can assess the risks Jensen Huang's departure poses to the broader
AI trade with our familiar framework of three constraints on the AI
investment boom.
(1) Financial constraints. NVIDIA has been described by many as the
central bank of the AI trade, conducting its own version of quantitative
easing by helping finance the expansion of AI computing capacity. The
company spent $42.2 billion purchasing equity securities during the first

half of its current fiscal year, compared with just $1.2 billion a year earlier.
The fear is that Huang's departure could slow or even end these financing
activities, tightening financial conditions for an industry already facing
questions about the returns on its enormous capital expenditures.

### Page 7

(2) Political constraints. Huang has also been the AI trade's biggest
cheerleader, tirelessly promoting its economic benefits and defending key
players. During October's AI jitters, for example, he publicly defended
CoreWeave's financial health amid a selloff in its shares and bonds,
helping restore confidence. His departure could deprive the industry of its
most influential advocate and cheerleader.
Additionally, NVIDIA reportedly intervened in the Commerce Department's

investigation into Megaspeed, while Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick
reportedly resisted efforts to blacklist the company, which remains off the
Entity List. Reports also suggest the Trump administration knew about
Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy. These allegations
could invite tougher congressional oversight and undermine Trump's
national security argument for building more data centers in the US,
making the AI buildout harder to sell to the public.

(3) Physical constraints. Washington could respond by tightening chip
export controls and closing loopholes that allow Chinese companies to
access NVIDIA-powered computing capacity remotely. If so, China might
retaliate by restricting exports of rare earth minerals, some of which are
crucial for building data centers. China has already warned Washington
against using the controversy as a pretext for imposing tougher
technology restrictions.
III. Nvidia: Our Base Case

Our base case is that NVIDIA's financing strategy remains intact. Colette
Kress, NVIDIA's longtime CFO and newly appointed interim CEO, has
worked closely with Huang since 2013 and helped oversee the company's
strategic investments and financing arrangements. During NVIDIA's
August 26 earnings call, she defended these investments against
accusations of circular financing, stating, "We recognize the scale of this
support, and we know some will call it circular financing. We see it

differently." She argued that these investments would generate attractive
returns while strengthening NVIDIA's AI ecosystem, suggesting continuity
under her leadership. We don't expect political constraints to meaningfully
increase as a result of the controversy. Nor do we expect another trade
conflict with China to erupt.

### Page 8

Even if some of the constraints intensify, they would likely turn existing
speed bumps into bigger speed bumps rather than roadblocks to the AI
investment boom. AI remains the latest evolution in the Digital Revolution
that began with IBM's mainframes, and we expect the investment boom to
continue without Huang (chart). We therefore view any resulting selloff as
another buying opportunity for the AI trade.

Spending resilient despite headwinds
4 November 2026
Key points
• Household spending rose 0.6%mth in
September, returning to growth after
August’s brief decline. Q3 spending increased
2.1%qtr, while volumes rose a more subdued

0.7%qtr, pointing to a sharp acceleration
in price growth through the quarter.
• Services spending rebounded and goods
spending remained supportive. Non-
discretionary spending outperformed
discretionary categories, with higher fuel
spending providing significant support.
• In real terms, spending growth over the
quarter was led by transport but with
most other categories also recording
gains. All states except Tasmania
recorded positive volume growth

in Q3, led by WA and Victoria.
• Today’s volumes outcome was broadly in
line with expectations and consistent with
our consumption outlook ahead of the Q3
National Accounts (due 2 December).

### Page 2

are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The monthly household spending indicator rose 0.6%mth in
September (nominal), exceeding both Westpac and market
expectations. We had anticipated a 0.5%mth contraction
(on the basis of a softer read on vehicle sales in the month),

while the market had pencilled in a 0.2%mth rise. Today’s
result instead marks a return to growth following August’s
brief contraction (revised down 0.1ppt to –0.1%mth), with
annual growth strengthening to 6.8%yr. For Q3 as a whole,
nominal spending rose 2.1%qtr, the strongest quarterly
result since the December quarter of 2022. In real terms,
volumes increased a more subdued 0.7%qtr, pointing to a
sharp acceleration in price growth through the quarter.

Non-discretionary spending outperforms
By broad category, services spending more than reversed
last month’s decline, rising a solid 1.1%mth (+6.8%yr). Goods
spending held around its recent pace, increasing 0.3%mth
(+6.7%yr). Discretionary spending returned to growth in
September, rising 0.3%mth (+6.9%yr), although this failed to
fully unwind August’s decline.
The relative strength in non-discretionary spending observed
last month continued into September, with the category rising

1.2%mth (+6.5%yr). While the headline result was stronger,
the breakdown aligns with findings from our Westpac–DataX
Card Tracker, and Westpac-DataX Consumer Panel which
show discretionary goods continuing to underperform other
categories (see here and here).
At the detailed level, transport and health led the gains,
both rising 1.5%mth. The increase in transport spending was
largely attributable to higher fuel spending, up 5.4%mth,
reflecting elevated fuel prices, with volumes down 2.2%mth.

The ABS also noted higher spending on vehicle repairs
and maintenance, while domestic air travel increased as
households travelled to attend major sporting events.
Recreation & culture rose 0.8%mth, while miscellaneous
goods & services (+0.6%mth), hotels, cafes & restaurants
(+0.5%mth), and clothing & footwear (+0.1%mth) also
recorded gains.
Partially offsetting these increases, food spending declined
0.1%mth, while furnishings & household equipment fell

0.2%mth, likely reflecting the ongoing weakness in housing
turnover (see here). Alcoholic beverages & tobacco recorded
the largest decline, falling 0.7%mth.
State outcomes were positive across the board in September.
WA led the gains, rising 1.1%mth and reversing last
month’s decline. Victoria also rebounded (+1.0%mth),
while Queensland (+0.6%mth), SA (+0.6%mth), Tasmania
(+0.5%mth) and NSW (+0.3%mth) all recorded increases.
Spending returns to growth in September

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Volume growth remains firm 
Nominal spending rose a firm 2.1%qtr in Q3, while volumes 
increased a more subdued 0.7%qtr, matching the pace recorded 
in Q2. This implies a sharp acceleration in price growth to 
1.4%qtr, from 0.6%qtr in Q2, and marks the strongest quarterly 
increase in prices since the December quarter of 2022. Through 
the year, volume growth strengthened to 3.2%yr, remaining 
well above the 1.8%yr average recorded through 2025.
Transport led gains in volume terms, rising 1.6%qtr, 
likely partly reflecting base effects in air travel following 
disruptions associated with the Middle East conflict in the June 
quarter. Clothing & footwear (+1.2%qtr), health (+1.1%qtr), 
miscellaneous goods & services (+1.1%qtr), food (+0.5%qtr), 
recreation & culture (+0.5%qtr), and furnishings & household 
equipment (+0.1%qtr) also recorded increases. Hotels, cafes 
& restaurants spending declined 0.1%qtr in volume terms, 
while alcoholic beverages & tobacco was the main drag, falling 
1.2%qtr.
Most states recorded real spending growth over the quarter, 
led by WA (+1.4%qtr), Victoria (+0.9%qtr), NSW (+0.5%qtr), 
Queensland (+0.5%qtr) and SA (+0.4%qtr). Tasmania lagged its 
state peers, recording a 0.1%qtr decline.
Conclusion
The household spending indicator provides a useful guide to 
around two-thirds of total consumer expenditure. Importantly, 
it excludes several major categories included in the National 
Accounts measure of real household consumption, including 
rents and utilities.

Today’s volumes outcome was broadly in line with our 
expectations, with Westpac anticipating around a 0.6%qtr rise 
in Q3, and remains consistent with our view on consumption 
ahead of the Q3 National Accounts (due 2 December). 
However, this will likely be the high water mark for spending. 
The renewed deterioration in global energy markets and the 
cumulative effect of four rate increases over the first three 
quarters of the year are likely to weigh more heaveily on 
household spending in Q4.

Looking further ahead, households will continue to face 
a restrictive monetary policy environment, with the RBA 
delivering an additional rate increase at yesterday’s November 
meeting (see here). Combined with persistent consumer 
pessimism, ongoing cost-of-living pressures (see here) and a 
housing downturn impacting household wealth, spending looks 
set to slow further in 2027.

## Document 403: New Zealand 2026 General Election_ Preliminary results and next steps (2026-11-16)

Source PDF: all report pdf/New Zealand 2026 General Election_ Preliminary results and next steps (2026-11-16).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-16
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1
Westpac New Zealand Economics
• As polls suggested, the election was a nailbiter
that has not yet been resolved. Election night
results show a lead to the centre-right on
paper in a significantly enlarged parliament.
• The centre-right bloc of National, NZ First
and ACT won 67 seats. The centre-left bloc
of Labour, Greens and Te Pāti Māori won 57
seats. There was a swing away from National
to its coalition partners while the centre left
grouping picked up a few seats.

• The Opportunity Party delivered on its polling
and will enter parliament with at least 7 seats,
reducing what could have been a larger swing
to the centre-left grouping.
• The size of parliament will expand by around 9
seats as National held onto a large share of its
electorate votes, entitling it to more seats than
implied by its party vote share. As a result,
a bloc would require 66 seats to command a
majority in parliament.
• Several senior National ministers have not

been re-elected, including Finance Minister
Nicola Willis. She will continue in a caretaker
role but will need to be replaced in any new
centre-right government.
• Significant uncertainty about the result exists.
National might lose perhaps two marginal
electorates in the final analysis. There are
400,000 special votes to count which may
shift the list seat allocations as these tend
to reduce the share of the centre-right bloc
by 1.6-1.7ppts. Hence, when all is said and

done, the centre-right’s 10 seat advantage on
election night could reduce by 1-4 seats.
• Should this play out the centre-right would not
have sufficient seats to reach a majority in an
enlarged house and would require the support
of the Opportunity Party to govern.
• The final election outcome will not be
known until 27 November. Serious coalition
negotiations likely won’t begin until then
and delays could occur. Should recounts be
required, some further delay is possible.

• Based on these results, we see a higher
probability of a centre-right government
ultimately emerging that likely will require
the support of the Opportunity Party in some
form. The Opportunity Party likely won’t enter
Cabinet but may offer support on confidence
and supply in exchange for policy concessions.
• But a centre-left government can’t be ruled
out if special votes heavily favour the centre
left bloc, and especially if an agreement
between the Opportunity Party and the centre-

right parties can’t be reached.
• Regardless of the final count, the results
suggest a government with weak support and
ongoing vulnerabilities. Hence the prospect of
controversial large changes may be modest.
For a centre-right government that might
limit the extent of fiscal consolidation, while
the centre left’s plans for larger scale tax and
spending initiatives could be trimmed back.
• A centre-right government won’t change the
RBNZ mandate while a centre-left government

likely still will. A new finance minister will be
required with the possibility of ACT leader
David Seymour taking on that role.
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
New Zealand 2026 General Election:
Preliminary results and next steps.
9 Nov 2026 |  Westpac Economics Team | westpac.co.nz/economics | economics@westpac.co.nz
Kelly Eckhold, Chief Economist | +64 9 348 9382 | +64 21 786 758 | kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
The result.
The preliminary results of the 2026 General Election,
based on the election night count, are set out in the table
above. In summary:
• The centre-right bloc of National, NZ First and ACT
won 67 seats.
• The centre-left bloc of Labour, Greens and Te Pāti
Māori won 57 seats.
• The Opportunity Party delivered on its polling and will
enter parliament with 7 seats.
These results indicate a swing away from National to its

coalition partners while the centre-left grouping picked
up a few seats. The big gainer was the Opportunity Party
who perhaps took a big share of what otherwise could
have been a larger swing to the centre-left grouping.
The size of parliament will expand by around nine seats,
as National held onto a large share of its electorate
votes and hence has more seats than it would have been
entitled to based on its party vote share. As a result, a
governing bloc would require 66 seats to command a

majority in parliament.
Several of the electorate seats are very tight (see Table
2), so election night majorities could easily be overturned
once special votes are counted. Of particular relevance
is the seat of Wairarapa, where the National candidate is
leading the Labour candidate by just 139 votes. If Labour
were to win that seat (specials favoured Labour in 2020
but National in 2023), all else equal the centre-right seat
count would decline to 66 seats in what would become

a 130-seat Parliament – just enough to command a
majority. But if either NZ First or ACT were to lose a single
party vote seat to the left bloc once specials are counted
then that majority would disappear.
On that score, historically the election night results have
tended to overstate the support of the centre-right bloc
and understate the support for parties on the left of
the political spectrum. For example, over the previous
three elections (2017, 2020, 2023), the current centre-

right bloc’s vote share has declined 1.9%, 1.7% and 1.6%
respectively following the counting of special votes, with
the National Party losing 2 seats on each occasion once
the official results were declared (Labour, the Greens and
Te Pāti Māori have benefited). On this occasion, given its
overhang, a lower party vote share would not lower the
National Party’s final vote count, although it could alter
the size of its overhang and thus the number of seats

required to command a majority. In recent elections both
NZ First and ACT have seen their vote shares decline
following the counting of special votes.
The process from here.
Official results.
The official results will be declared on 27 November,
adding the results of an estimated 400,000 special votes
(with around a quarter of these cast overseas) to the
2.41m votes counted on election night.
We expect discussions between potential coalition
partners will begin immediately to start sketching out the

parameters of a coalition agreement. But given the tight
margins of the current vote count, no firm positions will
be agreed until the results are declared.
The writ and recounts.
After 27 November, the writ is scheduled to be returned
on 3 December – formally certifying who has been
elected. However, that date is subject to any recounts

Westpac New Zealand Economics
that might be requested. Parliament is required to meet
no later than 14 January (six weeks after the return of the
writ), providing an opportunity for a new government
to test that it has the confidence of the House. It is
worth noting that Parliament can be summoned and
begin functioning even if coalition negotiations are still
occurring. If no coalition or confidence-and-supply
arrangement has been reached by then, negotiations can

simply continue and the incumbent Government remains
in office under the caretaker convention (Ministers in
the outgoing government retain executive authority
temporarily, even if they are no longer MPs).
Given that the Pre-Election Economic and Fiscal
Update was released in late September there is no legal
requirement for the Treasury to publish a Half Year
Economic and Fiscal Update (HYEFU) this year. And given
that it will be some weeks before a government is formed,

it seems unlikely that there will be a government in place
that would request that a HYEFU be produced despite
there being no statutory requirement (certainly there
would be little time to incorporate policy changes that
fall out of new coalition agreements, thus diminishing
its value). Once a government is formed, it will have an
opportunity to lay out its immediate priorities in the
Budget Policy Statement (BPS), which by law needs to
be released before the end of March next year. The new

government could decide to release an interim fiscal
update with the BPS if that was thought necessary.
Potential coalition outcomes.
We see three main likely outcomes:
• A three-party majority coalition between National, ACT
and NZ First.
• A three-party majority coalition between National, ACT
and NZ First with the support of the Opportunity Party
on confidence and supply.
• Some coalition of Labour, the Greens, Opportunity and
Te Pāti Māori, potentially with Opportunity and Te Pāti

Māori sitting outside the cabinet.
Our preview of the election discussed the policies of
the main political parties, while the figure on page 5
highlights some points of commonality with the centre-
right and centre-left blocs.
National, ACT and NZ First share a broadly pro-
development and deregulatory agenda, including support
for greater resource development, fewer regulatory
barriers and compulsory or expanded KiwiSaver. Beyond
that common core, National and ACT align particularly

closely on lower taxes and deregulation, while National
and NZ First share an emphasis on infrastructure, regional
development and law and order. ACT and NZ First favour
tighter immigration settings and tighter welfare policies.
The differences are clearest at the edges: ACT advocates
deeper tax cuts, smaller government and more market-
led provision, while NZ First places greater emphasis
on economic sovereignty, regional development and
targeted support for working New Zealanders. National

occupies more of the middle ground, combining fiscal
discipline and a pro-business agenda with increased
infrastructure and housing supply.
The Opportunity Party presents itself as a middle
alternative that can work with the left or the right
groupings. They have five bottom line policies they say
they need to support any new government. These include
competition reform through enhanced Commerce
Commission powers, more restrictions on political
lobbyists and donors, a ten-year plan of investment in the

healthcare sector, an end to bottom trawling in inshore
areas and a phase out elsewhere and for the Government
to recommit to New Zealand’s Paris Agreement
obligations. Their success in meeting these bottom
lines with a new government and their flexibility around
these requirements will be key to their ability to form an
agreement with either side. There is better congruence
of the Opportunity Party’s policies with the left than the
right – so progress here will be critical to watch should

their support be required to form a new government. We
suspect they will be very motivated to avoid the need for
a new election via a hung parliament should push come
to shove.

Labour, the Greens and Opportunity share support
for increased public and social investment, a broader
tax base, greater infrastructure investment, stronger
competition policy, cheaper public transport and a
more active role for government. Labour and the Greens
overlap particularly on higher taxation, public investment
and stronger climate and environmental policy, while
Labour and Opportunity share an emphasis on social

investment and higher public-service wages. The Greens
and Opportunity both favour broader taxation and
stronger climate action, although their preferred tax
instruments differ. Their distinctive priorities are also
clear: Labour places more weight on worker protections
and restoring the RBNZ’s dual mandate; the Greens on
environmental protection, progressive taxation and Te
Tiriti; and Opportunity on land-value taxation, housing
infrastructure, productivity, innovation and evidence-

based policy.

## Document 410: OpenAI推出ChatGPT钱包，AI智能体可代表用户购物付款
